 Page ES-1 of ES-4

This closure report (CR) presents information supporting the closure of Corrective Action Unit
(CAU) 572: Test Cell C Ancillary Building and Structures, Nevada National Security Site, Nevada.
This CR complies with the requirements of the Federal Facility Agreement and Consent Order
(FFACO), which was agreed to by the State of Nevada, U.S. Department of Energy (DOE), and
U.S. Department of Defense. CAU 572 comprises the five corrective action sites (CASs) listed
in Table ES-1.

 Table ES-1
 CAU 572 CASs and Corrective Actions
 CAS Number CAS Description Corrective Action

 25-33-01 Building 3220, Equipment Building No Further Action

 25-33-02 Building 3230, Motor Drive Building Clean Closure

 25-33-03 Building 3231, Pump House No Further Action

 25-33-04 Building 3232, Cryogenic Evaluation Lab Clean Closure

 a Aboveground Structures: Clean Closure
 25-99-22 Ancillary Facilities
 Below-Grade Structures/Utilities: Close in Place

 a
 Per the Streamlined Approach for Environmental Restoration (SAFER) Plan, includes (1) Dewars 1 and 2; (2) Dewars 4 and 5;
 (3) Borated Water, Shield, and Privy System; (4) Process Water Storage Tank and Boiler/Heater; and (5) Flare Stack Piping. The
 following were added in the CR: elevated water tower, below-grade vaults, and miscellaneous site infrastructure.

The purpose of this CR is to provide documentation supporting the completed corrective actions and
data confirming that the closure objectives for CAU 572 were met. To achieve this, the following
actions were performed:

 • Corrective action investigation (CAI) activities were performed from August 2020 through
 May 2023, as set forth in the SAFER Plan for CAU 572; and in accordance with the Soils
 Activity Quality Assurance Plan, which establishes requirements, technical planning, and
 general quality practices.

 • Corrective actions were completed during decontamination and demolition (D&D) and
 disposal activities, and were performed from August 2022 through September 2025.

 Page ES-2 of ES-4

The approach for the CAI was to investigate and make data quality objective (DQO) decisions based
on the types of releases present. The reporting of investigation results, the evaluation of DQO
decisions, and the evaluation of corrective action alternatives (CAAs) were applied at the FFACO
CAS level.

The purpose of the CAI was to fulfill data needs as defined during the DQO process. The CAU 572
dataset of investigation results was evaluated based on a data quality assessment. This assessment
demonstrated that the dataset is complete and acceptable for use in fulfilling the DQO data needs.

Investigation results were evaluated against final action levels (FALs) established in this document.
A radiological dose FAL of 25 millirem per year was established based on the Occasional Use
Area exposure scenario (80 hours of annual exposure). Radiological doses did not exceed the FAL at
any of the five CASs.

Based on the results of the investigation and samples collected (including visual inspections and
process knowledge), the following potential source material (PSM) was identified:

 • CAS 25-33-02, Building 3230, Motor Drive Building:

 - Lead contamination is present in the drain sediment at levels that exceed the FAL.

 • CAS 25-33-04, Building 3232, Cryogenic Evaluation Lab:

 - Arsenic, chromium, and lead contamination is present in the paint at levels that exceed
 the FALs.

 • CAS 25-99-22, Ancillary Facilities:

 - Arsenic contamination is present in the cooling tower sediment that exceeds the FAL.

 - Chromium and polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs) (Aroclor 1254 and Aroclor 1260)
 contamination is present in the paint from Dewar 1.

 - Semivolatile organic compounds (SVOCs) (benzo(a)pyrene, bis(2-ethylhexyl)phthalate)
 and PCBs (Aroclor 1254 and Aroclor 1260) contamination is present in the paint from the
 borated water storage tank.

 - Lead grout (metallic lead) was identified in cracks and joints of the concrete pad under and
 around the dewars.

 Page ES-3 of ES-4

 - Visual inspections of the below-grade vaults indicate the presence of hydrocarbon on some
 of the valves/pumps, and lead is present in drain sediment. Based on these observations, it
 is assumed that the below-grade structures (e.g., piping, valves, pumps) have possible
 hydrocarbon and lead as contaminants of concern (COCs).

 • Additional PSM was identified throughout the site to include mercury switches located in one
 of the vaults, spools of lead-wrapped wire, lead bricks, lead in building materials, electronics
 waste (e-waste), and other small quantities of waste associated with equipment and materials.

As there is no information available that would distinguish different paints used at the Test Cell C
(TCC) complex, it was conservatively assumed that the contaminants found in distinct paint chip
samples could potentially be present in paint for any of the other site infrastructure such as utility
poles, elevated water tower, equipment, and miscellaneous structures at TCC. Each of these items
was characterized and dispositioned accordingly.

The corrective actions implemented at CAU 572 were developed based on an evaluation of analytical
data from the CAI, the assumed presence of PSM, and the detailed and comparative analysis of the
CAAs. The CAAs were selected on technical merit focusing on performance, reliability, feasibility,
safety, and cost.

This CR provides documentation for closure of CAU 572 under the FFACO with corrective actions of
closure in place for the below-grade structures. Closure of CAU 572 consisted of the
following activities:

 • Characterize, remove, and dispose of all COCs identified in Buildings 3230 and 3232; and
 Ancillary Facilities (Dewars 1 and 2; Dewars 4 and 5; Borated Water, Shield, and Privy
 System; Process Water Storage Tank and Boiler/Heater; Flare Stack Piping; elevated water
 tower; below-grade vault; and miscellaneous site infrastructure).

 • Demolish the following buildings/structures: Buildings 3220, 3230, 3231, and 3232; and
 Ancillary Facilities (Dewars 1 and 2; Dewars 4 and 5; Borated Water, Shield, and Privy
 System; Process Water Storage Tank and Boiler/Heater; Flare Stack Piping; elevated water
 tower; and miscellaneous site infrastructure).

 • Remove miscellaneous debris (e.g., equipment/trailers, building rubble [cinder blocks, metal,
 wood, construction debris]), and industrial and hazardous waste located within and
 immediately adjacent to the TCC fence.

 • The below-grade vaults were filled in with clean earthen material and grouted to the surface
 except for the below-grade void space under Building 3230, Motor Drive Building. This void

 Page ES-4 of ES-4

 space was partially filled with non-radioactive demolition debris (concrete rubble) from
 Building 3231, Pump House, topped with clean earthen material, and grouted to surface.

 • Implement an FFACO use restriction (UR) for CAS 25-99-22 for hydrocarbon and lead
 assumed present in the underground piping associated with the below-grade vaults and
 utilities, plus the lead grout (metallic lead) identified in the surface cracks and joints of the
 concrete pads where the dewars once stood. The UR boundary is extended to the TCC fence
 line to capture all of the underground piping (left in place), which is associated with the
 below-grade vaults.

Corrective-actions-generated waste streams include the following: industrial solid waste, hazardous
waste, mixed low-level waste, and low-level waste. Waste minimization activities included
segregation of waste streams, recycling, and size reduction. Waste disposition documentation is
included as Appendix E of this report, and any residual waste disposition will be documented in an
addendum, as needed.

References
Federal Facility Agreement and Consent Order. 1996 (as amended March 2010 and July 2025).
 Agreed to by the State of Nevada, U.S. Department of Energy, and U.S. Department of Defense.

Navarro. 2022. Soils Activity Quality Assurance Plan, N/0002653--096. Las Vegas, NV.

 Building and Structures, Nevada National Security Site, Nevada, Rev. 0, DOE/EMNV--0026.

 Page 1 of 48

1.0 Introduction

This closure report (CR) presents information supporting closure of Corrective Action Unit
(CAU) 572, Test Cell C Ancillary Building and Structures, located at the Nevada National Security
Site (NNSS), Nevada, as shown in Figure 1-1. CAU 572 comprises the five corrective action sites
(CASs) shown on Figure 1-2 and listed below:

 • 25-33-01, Building 3220, Equipment Building
 • 25-33-02, Building 3230, Motor Drive Building
   •   25-33-03, Building 3231, Pump House
 • 25-33-04, Building 3232, Cryogenic Evaluation Lab
 • 25-99-22, Ancillary Facilities

A detailed discussion of the history of this CAU is presented in the Streamlined Approach for
Environmental Restoration (SAFER) Plan for Corrective Action Unit 572: Test Cell C Ancillary
Building and Structures (DOE/EMNV, 2021). The need for corrective action and corrective action
alternatives (CAAs) is evaluated separately for each CAS.

The Federal Facility Agreement and Consent Order (FFACO) CAS descriptions listed above may
also be referred to throughout legacy documents as follows:

   •   25-33-01: Building 3220
   •   25-33-02: Building 3230
   •   25-33-03: Building 3231
   •   25-33-04: Building 3232

Per the SAFER Plan, CAS 25-99-22, Ancillary Facilities consists of the following:

 • Dewars 1 and 2
 • Dewars 4 and 5
 • Borated Water, Shield, and Privy System
 • Process Water Storage Tank and Boiler/Heater
 • Flare Stack Piping

1.1 Purpose

The purpose of this CR is to provide documentation and justification that no further corrective action
is needed for the closure of CAU 572 based on the implementation of corrective actions.
This includes a description of investigation activities, an evaluation of the data, and a description
of corrective actions that were performed. This CR also provides the analytical data to confirm that
the remediation goals were met. The SAFER provides information relating to the scope and planning
of the investigation. Therefore, that information will not be repeated in this document.

1.2 Scope

The CAI for CAU 572 was completed by demonstrating through environmental soil and material
sample analytical results the nature and extent of contaminants of concern (COCs). For radiological
releases, COCs are defined as radionuclides that jointly present a dose to a receptor exceeding a final
action level (FAL) of 25 millirem per year (mrem/yr). For chemical releases, a COC is defined as a
contaminant in soil that is present above its corresponding FAL.

The objectives for closure included the following activities:

 • Regulated or hazardous materials will be removed and dispositioned in accordance with
 applicable regulations.

 • The aboveground structures will be demolished and dispositioned (i.e., removed from TCC).

 • The below-grade vaults not previously filled will be partially filled with clean earthen
 material and filled to the surface with flowable grout unless noted.

All activities were completed in accordance with the SAFER and its ensuing Record of Technical
Change (ROTC) (DOE/EMNV, 2021 and 2025); and in accordance with the Soils Activity Quality
Assurance Plan (QAP) (DOE/EMNV, 2017; Navarro, 2022), which establishes requirements,
technical planning, and general quality practices. The investigation results and the risk associated
with site contamination were evaluated in accordance with the Soils Risk-Based Corrective Action
(RBCA) Evaluation Process (DOE/EMNV, 2018).

 Page 6 of 48

The activities at TCC were performed in two phases. Phase 1, the CAI, was followed by Phase 2
activities to include decontamination and demolition (D&D) and closure activities. The following
CAI activities were completed in Phase 1:

 • Performed visual surveys to identify biasing factors for potential soil and PSM
 sample locations.

 • Performed radiological surveys to identify biasing factors for potential soil and PSM
 sample locations.

 • Collected soil samples at the biased locations and PSM.

 • Submitted samples for analysis.

 • Removed ballasts and e-waste.

 • Conducted waste management activities (e.g., sampling, disposal).

 • Evaluated corrective action objectives based on the results of the CAI and the CAA
 screening criteria.

The following activities were performed as part of Phase 2 to include the D&D and closure activities:

 • Removed and dispositioned oils (polychlorinated biphenyl [PCB] and non-PCB) from
 hydraulic systems and equipment.

 • Removed hazardous and regulated materials, including the following:

 - Lead (electrical components, batteries, debris)

 - Mercury (switches)

 - Asbestos-containing material (ACM) (insulation, piping, electrical cabinet heat shields,
 equipment, and asphaltic roofing products)

 - PCBs (fluids/lubricants, PCB bulk product material)

 - Freon (heating, venting, and air conditioning systems)

 • Removed the aboveground structures including those associated with CASs identified in
 Section 1.0 and other site infrastructure such as the elevated water tower and miscellaneous
 TCC area equipment, structures (e.g., substation, utility poles).

 Page 7 of 48

 • Filled in the below-grade vaults with clean earthen material and grouted to the surface with
 the exception of the below-grade void space under Building 3230, Motor Drive Building. This
 void space was backfilled with non-radioactive demolition debris (concrete rubble) from
 Building 3231, Pump House.

 • Installed use restriction (UR) signs, and recorded the URs in the FFACO database; the
 Management and Operating (M&O) Contractor Geographic Information Systems (GIS); and
 the Environmental Management (EM) Nevada Program CAU/CAS files.

1.3.2 Data Quality Objectives

This section contains a summary of the DQO process presented in Appendix A. The DQOs were
developed to identify data needs, clearly define the intended use of the environmental data, and
design a data collection program that will satisfy these purposes.

The problem statement for CAU 572 is: “Existing information on the nature and extent of potential
contamination is insufficient to validate the assumptions used to select the corrective actions or to
verify that closure objectives were met for the CASs in CAU 572.” To address this question, the
resolution of two decisions statements is required:

 • Decision I: “Is any COC present or is any material present that exceeds PSM criteria as a
 result of CAU 572 releases?”

 Page 9 of 48

 • Decision II: “Is sufficient information available to confirm that closure objectives were met?”
 Sufficient information is defined to include the following:

 - Identifying the volume of material containing any COC or PSM
 - The information needed to determine remediation waste types

The presence of a COC would require a corrective action. A corrective action might also be necessary
if there is a potential for wastes present at a site to result in the introduction of COCs into site
environmental media. These wastes would be considered PSM, which is defined as waste (solid or
liquid) containing contaminants that, if released to soil, would result in soil contamination exceeding
a FAL. For purposes of this document, PSM may be referred to as a COC.

The criteria to be used for determining whether a waste is PSM are defined in the Soils RBCA
document (DOE/EMNV, 2018).

1.3.3 Data Quality Assessment Summary

The SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) contains the DQOs as agreed to by decision makers before the field
investigation. The DQO process ensures that the right type, quality, and quantity of data will be
available to support the resolution of those decisions with an appropriate level of confidence. A data
quality assessment (DQA) was conducted that evaluated the degree of acceptability and usability of
the reported data in the decision-making process. This DQA is presented in Section 4.1. Using both
the DQO and DQA processes helps to ensure that DQO decisions are sound and defensible.

Based on this evaluation, the nature and extent of COCs at CAU 572 have been adequately identified
to implement the corrective actions. Information generated during the investigation supports the
conceptual site model (CSM) assumptions, and the data collected met the DQOs and support their
intended use in the decision-making process.

                                                                                         Page 10 of 48

2.0 Closure Activities

The following subsections summarize the CAU 572 closure activities and any deviations from the
original scope of work. Results of confirmation sampling for individual CAU 572 CASs are presented
in Appendix D of this document.

2.1 Description of CAI Activities

CAI activities were conducted from August 2020 through May 2023. The purpose of the
CAU 572 CAI was to provide the additional information needed to resolve the following
CAU 572-specific DQOs:

 • Determine whether COCs are present in the soils associated with CAU 572.

 • Determine the extent of identified COCs.

 • Ensure that adequate data have been collected to evaluate closure alternatives under
 the FFACO.

Data to calculate radiological dose were provided by the analytical results of soil samples. Data to
evaluate chemical risk were provided by analytical results of soil samples.

For DQO Decision I at potential release sites, sample locations were established judgmentally based
on the presence of biasing factors (e.g., lead bricks and highest radiation survey values). Using the
judgmental locations of highest potential contamination provides a conservative estimate of the
contaminant exposure a receptor would receive from working at the release site.

For DQO Decision II (the extent of COC contamination), the extent of metallic lead PSM was
determined as the physical extent of the metallic lead. The extent of other chemical contamination
found in aboveground structures was determined as the physical extent of structures in which PSM
was identified. For below-grade structures, the horizontal extent included the underground piping
associated with the below-grade vaults and was determined as the fence line surrounding the facility.

                                                                                        Page 11 of 48

As described in Appendix H, the total effective dose (TED) to a receptor from site contamination is a
function of the time the receptor is present at the site and exposed to the radioactively contaminated
soil. Therefore, TED is reported in this document based on the following three exposure scenarios
that address the potential exposure of industrial workers to contaminants in soil:

 • Industrial Area. Assumes continuous industrial use of a site. This scenario assumes that this
 is the regular assigned work area for the worker who will be on the site for an entire career
 (8 hours per day [hr/day], 250 days per year [day/yr] for 25 years). The TED values
 calculated using this exposure scenario are the TED an industrial area worker receives during
 2,000 hours of annual exposure to site radioactivity and are expressed in terms of millirem per
 Industrial Area year (mrem/IA-yr).

 • Remote Work Area. Assumes noncontinuous work activities at a site. This scenario assumes
 this is an area where the worker regularly visits but is not an assigned work area where the
 worker spends an entire workday. A site worker under this scenario is assumed to be on the
 site for an equivalent of 336 hours per year (hr/yr) (or 8 hr/day for 42 day/yr) for an entire
 career (25 years). The TED values calculated using this exposure scenario are the TED a
 remote area worker receives during 336 hours of annual exposure to site radioactivity and are
 expressed in terms of millirem per Remote Work Area year (mrem/RW-yr).

 • Occasional Use Area. Assumes occasional work activities at a site. This scenario addresses
 industrial workers who are not assigned to the area as a regular worksite but may occasionally
 use the site. This scenario assumes this is an area where the worker does not regularly visit but
 may occasionally use for short-term activities. A site worker under this scenario is assumed to
 be on the site for an equivalent of 80 hr/yr (or 8 hr/day for 10 day/yr) for 5 years. The TED
 values calculated using this exposure scenario are the TED an occasional use worker receives
 during 80 hours of annual exposure to site radioactivity and are expressed in terms of millirem
 per Occasional Use Area year (mrem/OU-yr).

In accordance with the graded approach described in the Soils QAP (DOE/EMNV, 2017; Navarro,
2022), the dataset quality will be determined based on its intended use in decision making. Data used
to define the presence of COCs are classified as decisional and will be used to make corrective action
decisions. Survey data are classified as decision-supporting and are not used, by themselves, to make
corrective action decisions. As presented in Appendix H, the radiological FALs are based on the
Industrial Area exposure scenario, and the chemical FALs are based on the Occasional Use Area
site-specific exposure scenario.

The following subsections describe specific investigation activities conducted at each CAS.

2.1.1   CAS 25-33-01, Building 3220, Equipment Building

Investigation activities at CAS 25-33-01 included performing visual inspections, conducting
radiological surveys, and collecting PSM samples. During the visual inspections, biasing factors were
identified. The radiological surveys were conducted to identify locations of elevated radiological
readings that would indicate the locations of higher radiological activity. Results of the sampling
effort are reported in Section D.3.3.

The contamination at CAS 25-33-01 is consistent with the CSM and validates the CSM as presented
in the SAFER. No modification to the CSM was needed.

2.1.2 CAS 25-33-02, Building 3230, Motor Drive Building

Investigation activities at CAS 25-33-02 included performing visual inspections, conducting
radiological surveys, and collecting PSM samples. During the visual inspections, biasing factors were
identified. The radiological surveys were conducted to identify locations of elevated radiological
readings that would indicate the locations of higher radiological activity. Results of the sampling
effort are reported in Section D.4.3.

The contamination at CAS 25-33-02 is consistent with the CSM and validates the CSM as presented
in the SAFER. No modification to the CSM was needed.

2.1.3   CAS 25-33-03, Building 3231, Pump House

Investigation activities at CAS 25-33-03 included performing visual inspections and conducting
radiological surveys. During the visual inspections, biasing factors were not identified; therefore,
samples were not collected (as noted in Section D.5.3).

No modification to the CSM was needed.

In addition to the items described in the SAFER for CAS 25-99-22, other investigation activities were
conducted for the site infrastructure and included the elevated water tower, below-grade vaults, and
other miscellaneous TCC area equipment and structures (e.g., substation, utility poles).

2.2 Description of Closure, D&D, and Verification Activities

Corrective actions for CAU 572 included the removal and disposition of aboveground structures
(i.e., clean closure) and closure in place of below-grade piping and utilities. Visual and radiological

                                                                                        Page 14 of 48

surveys were utilized to verify clean closure (i.e., removal of PSM). Additionally, due to the
demolition methodology:

 • Nine closure verification samples were collected from surface soils in the footprint of where
 the water tank (from the elevated water tower) fell.

 • Two closure verification soil samples were taken from surface soils in the footprint of where
 the Process Water Storage Tank fell, was subsequently processed (size reduced), and
 was removed.

 • One composite closure verification sample was collected from the surface soil beneath
 lead-wire cable spools identified outside the TCC fence line.

Soil samples were field screened for alpha and beta/gamma radiation and shipped to offsite
laboratories for analyses. Visual inspections and analytical results confirmed that no residues were
left behind. Results of the verification sampling effort are reported in Tables D.7-8 and D.7-9. The
following subsections describe how closure activities were completed for CAU 572.

2.2.1 Pre-Planning and Site Preparation

Pre-planning and site preparation activities were completed before beginning closure activities. The
following documents were prepared before start of field activities:

 • National Environmental Policy Act checklist
 • Real Estate/Operations Permit
 • Worker Health and Safety Documentation
 • Field Work Control Documentation

2.2.2 Site Biological Survey

A pre-job biological survey of the site was performed in November 2022. The field survey identified
one old tortoise carcass inside the fenced compound (natural death) and one inactive bird nest.
Subsequent inspections performed between November 2022 and January 2025 identified three bird
carcasses (all appeared to be natural deaths), and one abandoned nest with three eggs. All other nests
identified were avoided throughout closure activities until chicks fledged or were removed before
becoming active. All migratory birds found dead were reported to the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service
through the Injury and Mortality Reporting system. All workers at the site were briefed of the
presence of the nests, and the nesting areas were avoided during work activities.

                                                                                        Page 15 of 48

2.2.3 Installation of Temporary Facilities and Utilities

Before beginning field activities, a temporary portable double-wide office trailer, internet, and
temporary facilities such as portable restrooms and hand-washing stations were installed at the
CAU 572 site outside the TCC boundary. Temporary power was provided via a mobile generator.

2.2.4 Hantavirus Cleanup

Potential hantavirus-bearing rodent droppings were treated, removed, and disposed. Hantavirus waste
was surveyed and dispositioned. Hantavirus cleanups were performed routinely throughout the
performance of field activities.

2.2.5 Radiological Surveys

Radiological surveys were performed at various locations within CAU 572. Radiological surveys
were performed to identify the presence, nature, and extent of radiological contaminants at areas
statistically distinguishable from background areas. Various radiological walkover surveys were
conducted at each CAS. The surveys included the interior and exterior of each building, each major
piece of equipment, support structures, and interior and exterior of piping. Radiological walkover
surveys were performed using an NE Technology Electra fitted with a DP6BD dual-alpha and
beta/gamma radiation probe.

2.2.6 Isolated and Sealed Utility Systems and Floor Drains

Utilities providing service to the facility during site operations were disconnected and validated as
out of service by the NNSS M&O contractor. The disconnected utilities included main water inlets as
well as the electrical transmission and distribution power lines. Systems transitioning through
building and area foundations were cut relatively flush with the foundation and were filled with
grout or equal material to act as a seal preventing contaminants from either entering or exiting the
isolated system. Systems include, but are not limited to, electrical conduits, process piping, and
surface/floor drains.

                                                                                       Page 16 of 48

2.2.7 Collection of Building Media/Oil Samples

As part of the investigation and closure processes, samples and swipe samples were collected for both
radiological and chemical analysis. Radiological samples/surveys were performed before and
throughout closure activities to determine the presence/absence of radiological contamination, ensure
that radiological contamination has not migrated from the known contamination areas, monitor
worker exposure, and determine waste disposition.

In June 2020, a detailed environmental inspection of suspect building materials was conducted to
identify ACM, lead-based paint, and beryllium surface dust on the interior and exterior of the TCC
facilities (Navarro, 2021).

Other types of building media collected and disposed of included motor oils/gear oils, hydraulic oils,
ballast capacitors, concrete, wood, and metal.

2.2.8 Removal of Non-Hazardous and Hazardous/Regulated Components

Electrical and lighting components known to contain regulated materials (i.e., mercury switches,
thermometers, batteries, mercury vapor light bulbs) were removed from Buildings 3220, 3230, 3231,
and 3232, and ancillary equipment. These components were determined to not be radiologically
impacted and were dispositioned accordingly.

Non-hazardous electrical and lighting components (e.g., conduit, cables, wiring, fuse boxes, motors,
control panels, incandescent bulbs) within Buildings 3220, 3230, 3231, and 3232, and ancillary
equipment were surveyed and determined to meet the Area 9 U10c Landfill acceptance criteria. Other
non-hazardous components were demolished and dispositioned with the facility structures, systems,
and components.

2.2.9 Asbestos Abatement

All regulated friable ACM was removed from the TCC facilities for safety and logistic reasons. The
ACM was identified as a component of the Process Water Storage Tank and Boiler/Heater, which was
packaged and disposed of as a single unit (Figure 2-1). The ACM was identified within the gasket
material as well as insulation wrapping within the unit. An engineering evaluation was performed on

products) was collected/disposed before and during demolition activities.

instrumentation, and building components in contact with and near the concrete foundation interface.

demolished around the motor for ease of access (Figure 2-2). Then the motor and turbine were
demolished in placed, packaged, and transported to the Area 9 U10c Landfill for disposal in
accordance with NNSS acceptance criteria. A concrete pit, approximately 7 feet (ft) deep and

2.2.12 Entombment (Grout Fill) of Below-Grade Vaults and Other
 Subsurface Features

A site visit by the DOE, National Nuclear Security Administration Nevada Field Office (NNSA/NFO)
and NDEP was conducted before entombment of subsurface structures.

For each below-grade void, with the exception of the below-grade void space of Building 3230,
Motor Drive Building, the space was first filled with new Class II road base, and then the last 12 to
18 inches was capped with the flowable grout as noted in Figure 2-12 and pictured in Figure 2-13.
The below-grade void of Building 3230, Motor Drive Building was partially filled with
non-radioactive demolition debris (concrete rubble) from Building 3231, Pump House followed by
new Class II road base, and then the last 12 to 18 inches was capped with the flowable grout. The
consistency of the grout was measured each day to ensure the flowability and minimize void spaces
within the structure. Each load of grout mixture consisted of the following:

   •   2,200 lbs of sand
 • 719 lbs of cement
 • 61 gal of water
 • Air (20 to 30 percent)

2.2.13 Final Postings and Markings

A final walk-through consisting of visual and radiological surveys of the facility was conducted to

3.0 Waste Disposition

This section addresses the characterization and management of investigation and remediation wastes.
Waste management activities were conducted as specified in the SAFER and its ensuing ROTC
(DOE/EMNV, 2021 and 2025).

3.1 Generated Wastes

The wastes listed in Table 3-1 were generated during CAI field investigation and D&D activities.
Wastes were segregated to the greatest extent possible, and waste minimization techniques
were integrated into the field activities to reduce the amount of waste generated. Controls were in
place to minimize the use of hazardous materials and the unnecessary generation of hazardous
and/or mixed waste.

The amount, type, and source of waste placed into each container were recorded in waste
management logbooks that are maintained in the CAU 572 file. In addition, the below-grade void of
Building 3230, Motor Drive Building was partially backfilled with approximately 35 cubic yards
(yd3) of non-radioactive demolition debris (i.e., concrete rubble) generated from the demolition of the
north and east wall of Building 3231, Pump House.

3.2 Waste Characterization and Disposal

All waste dispositions were based on process knowledge, radiological surveys, and samples, when
necessary. Waste characterization and disposition was based on federal and state regulations, permit
limitations, and disposal facility acceptance criteria. The executed waste shipping and disposal
documentation for CAU 572 is in Appendix E.

3.2.1 Industrial Solid Waste

ACMs were generated, characterized as industrial solid waste, and transferred to the Area 9 U10c
Landfill for disposal.

Friable and nonfriable ACMs consisting of pipe insulation, ceiling and floor tiles, and mastics were
generated and characterized as industrial solid waste that meets the chemical and radiological waste
acceptance criteria of the Area 9 U10c solid waste landfill.

ACMs were characterized by analysis of samples utilizing polarized light microscopy.

PCB bulk products greater than or equal to 50 parts per million (ppm) were identified in building
materials such as paints and caulking, and are a component of industrial solid waste.

Building structures and equipment that were not characterized as hazardous waste, MLLW, or LLW
were dispositioned as industrial solid waste and disposed in the Area 9 U10c Landfill with the
exception of the below-grade void of Building 3230, Motor Drive Building, which was partially
backfilled with approximately 35 yd3 of non-radioactive demolition debris (concrete rubble).-

3.2.2 Hazardous Waste

Minimal hazardous waste was generated, characterized, and transferred to the M&O contractor for
packaging and disposal at an offsite facility. Hazardous waste included paint chips, e-waste, lead,
lead-wrapped wire, used oil, mercury, and other miscellaneous chemicals removed from the area.

3.2.3 Mixed Low-Level Waste (MLLW)

Minimal radiological contaminated equipment, tools, and e-waste was characterized as MLLW and
transferred to the M&O contractor for disposal at the Area 5 RWMC.

                                                                                    Page 31 of 48

3.2.4 Low-Level Waste (LLW)

Legacy radiologically contaminated structures, equipment, tools, and personal protective equipment
(PPE) was generated, including small amounts of ACM and PCB bulk product. These materials
were managed as LLW and meet the waste acceptance criteria for disposal at the Area 3 RWMS and
Area 5 RWMC.

                                                                                          Page 32 of 48

4.0 Closure Verification Results

Closure verification results consist of the analytical results from environmental samples that
demonstrate that closure objectives were met. The SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) identified that the
right type, quality, and quantity of data are needed to resolve the DQO decision statements. To verify
that the dataset obtained as a result of this investigation supports the DQO decisions, a DQA was
conducted. Section 4.1 provides a summary of the DQA.

This section provides a summary of verification data from the closure activities as detailed in
Appendix D. Except as noted in the following CAS-specific sections, CAU 572 sampling locations
were accessible and sampling activities at planned locations were not restricted by buildings, storage
areas, active operations, or aboveground and underground utilities. The following subsections
provide a summary of the CAS-specific verification results as presented in Appendix D.

4.1 Data Quality Assessment

The DQA process is the scientific evaluation of the actual investigation results to determine whether
the DQO criteria established in the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) were met and whether DQO
decisions can be resolved at the desired level of confidence. The DQO process ensures that the right
type, quality, and quantity of data will be available to support the resolution of those decisions at an
appropriate level of confidence. Using both the DQO and DQA processes helps to ensure that DQO
decisions are sound and defensible.

The DQA involves five steps that begin with a review of the DQOs and end with an answer to the
DQO decisions. These steps are briefly summarized as follows:

1. Review DQOs and Sampling Design. Review the DQO process to provide context for
 analyzing the data. State the primary statistical hypotheses; confirm the limits on decision
 errors for committing false-negative (Type I) or false-positive (Type II) decision errors; and
 review any special features, potential problems, or any deviations to the sampling design.

2. Conduct a Preliminary Data Review. A preliminary data review should be performed by
 reviewing quality assurance (QA) reports and inspecting the data both numerically and
 graphically, validating and verifying the data to ensure that the measurement systems
 performed in accordance with the criteria specified, and using the validated dataset to
 determine whether the quality of the data is satisfactory.

                                                                                         Page 33 of 48

3. Select the Test. Select the test based on the population of interest, population parameter, and
 hypotheses. Identify the key underlying assumptions that could cause a change in one of the
 DQO decisions.

4. Verify the Assumptions. Perform tests of assumptions. If data are missing or censored,
 determine the impact on DQO decision error.

5. Draw Conclusions from the Data. Perform the calculations required for the test.

4.1.1 Review DQOs and Sampling Design

This section contains a review of the DQO process presented in Appendix A. The DQO decisions are
presented with the DQO provisions to limit false-negative and false-positive decision errors. Special
features, potential problems, or any deviations to the sampling design are also presented.

The preliminary action levels (PALs) and FALs for radioactivity are based on an annual dose limit of
25 mrem/yr. This dose limit is specific to the annual dose a receptor could potentially receive from a
CAU 572 release. As such, it is dependent upon the cumulative annual hours of exposure to site
contamination. The PALs for radioactivity were established in the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) based
on a dose limit of 25 mrem/yr over an annual exposure time of 2,000 hours (i.e., the Industrial Area
exposure scenario that a site worker would be exposed to site contamination 8 hr/day for 250 day/yr).
The FALs for radioactivity were established based on a dose limit of 25 mrem/yr over an annual
exposure time of 80 hours (i.e., the Occasional Use Area exposure scenario defines that a site worker
would be exposed to site contamination 8 hr/day for 10 day/yr).

The chemical PALs are based on the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) Regional
Screening Levels (RSLs) for chemical contaminants in industrial soils (EPA, 2020) except where
natural background concentrations of Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) metal
exceed the screening level (e.g., arsenic on the NNSS). The chemical FALs are discussed in
Appendix H. The chemical FALs were established based on an annual exposure time of 80 hours
(i.e., the Occasional Use Area exposure scenario defines that a site worker would be exposed to site
contamination 8 hr/day for 10 day/yr).

                                                                                          Page 34 of 48

4.1.2 Decision I

The Decision I statement as presented in the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) is as follows: “Are any
COCs present or is any waste present at the site likely to result in the introduction of COCs into site
environmental media?” Any contaminant present (or assumed to be present) at concentrations
exceeding its corresponding FAL will be defined as a COC. A COC may also be defined as a
contaminant that, in combination with other like contaminants, is determined to jointly pose an
unacceptable risk based on a multiple contaminant analysis (DOE/EMNV, 2018). If a COC is
detected, then Decision II must be resolved.

4.1.2.1 DQO Provisions To Limit False-Negative Decision Error

A false-negative decision error (when it is concluded that contamination exceeding FALs is not
present when it actually is) was controlled by meeting the following criteria:

 1) For Decision I, having a high degree of confidence that sample locations
 selected will identify COCs if present anywhere within the CAS
 (judgmental sampling).

 2) Having a high degree of confidence that analyses conducted will be sufficient to
 detect any COCs present in the samples.

 3) Having a high degree of confidence that the dataset is of sufficient quality
 and completeness.

Criteria 2 and 3 were assessed based on the entire dataset. Therefore, these assessments apply to both
Decision I and Decision II.

Criterion 1 (Confidence Judgmental Sample Locations Identify COCs)

To resolve Decision I (determine whether a COC is present at a release), samples were collected and
analyzed following these two criteria:

 • Samples must be collected in areas most likely to contain a COC.
 • The analytical suite selected must be sufficient to identify any COCs present in the samples.

To satisfy the criteria that the samples must be collected in areas most likely to contain a COC,
judgmental sample locations were selected at each CAS as follows (per DQOs versus actual
Decision I sampling).

                                                                                        Page 35 of 48

CAS 25-33-01, Building 3220, Equipment Building

The DQOs specified the following criteria for locations most likely to contain a COC, if present:

 “Two locations will be selected to collect a paint sample from the locations

 and not plugged, a sample of any material in the piping will be collected, if possible.”

For CAS 25-33-01, Building 3220, paint chip samples were collected at the two biased locations
determined to have the most deterioration. No soil was present below the sampled locations;
therefore, soil samples were not collected. Sinks and drains were present in the building, but very
little sediment was present. Therefore, sediment was composited from all of the sinks and drains to
have sufficient material for analysis. In addition, samples were collected from a pan containing black
sludge, concrete chips, and roofing material at two locations.

Therefore, all of the sampling objectives specified in the DQOs were met for CAS 25-33-01,
Building 3220, Equipment Building.

CAS 25-33-02, Building 3230, Motor Drive Building

The DQOs specified the following criteria for locations most likely to contain a COC, if present:

 “Two locations will be selected to collect a paint sample from the locations

 and not plugged, a sample of any material in the piping will be collected, if possible.”

At Building 3230, sufficient paint was not present for two samples. Scraping of the surface at various
locations was required to produce enough material for a composite sample. No soil was present below
the sampled locations; therefore, soil samples were not collected. Sinks were not present in the
building and very little sediment was present in the drains. Therefore, sediment was composited from
all of the drains to have sufficient material for analysis.

Therefore, all of the sampling objectives specified in the DQOs were met for Building 3230.

                                                                                        Page 36 of 48

CAS 25-33-03, Building 3231, Pump House

The DQOs specified the following criteria for locations most likely to contain a COC, if present:

 “Two locations will be selected to collect a paint sample from the locations

 and not plugged, a sample of any material in the piping will be collected, if possible.
 A radiation screening survey will be conducted inside the building over the areas
 most likely to be contaminated (floors, walls, and piping in the vicinity of process
 equipment). If anomalous readings above background are detected, external dose will
 be measured and/or the area will be investigated for the presence of PSM.”

At Building 3231, (a concrete structure), there was no paint present to sample. Sinks and drains were
also not present, and no anomalous readings were found during the radiation screening. Therefore, no
samples were collected at this CAS.

Therefore, all of the sampling objectives specified in the DQOs were met for Building 3231.

CAS 25-33-04, Building 3232, Cryogenic Evaluation Lab

The DQOs specified the following criteria for locations most likely to contain a COC, if present:

 “Two locations will be selected to collect a paint sample from the locations

 and not plugged, a sample of any material in the piping will be collected, if possible.
 A radiation screening survey will be conducted inside the building over the areas
 most likely to be contaminated (floors, walls, and piping in the vicinity of process
 equipment). If anomalous readings above background are detected, external dose will
 be measured and/or the area will be investigated for the presence of PSM.”

At Building 3232, sufficient paint was not present for two samples. Scraping of the surface at various
locations was required to produce enough material for a composite sample. No soil was present below
the sampled locations; therefore, soil samples were not collected. Sinks and drains were also not
present and no anomalous readings were found during the radiation screening. Therefore, no
sediment samples or radiological samples were collected at this CAS.

Therefore, all of the sampling objectives specified in the DQOs were met for Building 3232.

                                                                                        Page 37 of 48

CAS 25-99-22, Ancillary Facilities

Per the SAFER Plan, CAS 25-99-22, Ancillary Facilities consists of the following:

 • Dewars 1 and 2
 • Dewars 4 and 5
 • Borated Water, Shield, and Privy System
 • Process Water Storage Tank and Boiler/Heater
 • Flare Stack Piping

For Dewars 1, 2, 4, and 5 and the Borated Water, Shield, and Privy System, the DQOs specified the
following criteria for locations most likely to contain a COC, if present:

 “Two locations will be selected to collect a paint sample from the locations

 and not plugged, a sample of any material in the piping will be collected, if possible.
 A radiation screening survey will be conducted inside the building over the areas
 most likely to be contaminated (cement pad, walls, and piping in the vicinity of
 process equipment). If anomalous readings above background are detected, external
 dose will be measured and/or the area will be investigated for the presence of PSM.”

At Dewars 1, 2, 4, and 5, two paint chip samples were collected from the dewars. Concrete surfaces
are present under the dewars; therefore, no soil samples were collected. No sinks or drains were
present, and no anomalous radiological readings were found during the radiation screening.

Scraping of the painted surfaces of the Borated Water Storage Tank and the water conditioner tanks of
the Privy System at various locations was required to produce enough material for two composite
paint chip samples. For the Borated Water, Shield, and Privy System, the DQOs specified that a
sample will be collected from the sediment within each of the cooling towers. A sample will also be
collected from the water softener resin.

A sediment sample was collected for each of the cooling tower bases as specified. The water softener
did not contain any resin-like material; however, a sample was collected from loose material in the
bottom of the water softener. No sinks or drains were present at this CAS, and no anomalous
radiological readings were found during the radiation screening.

                                                                                          Page 38 of 48

For the Process Water Storage Tank and Boiler/Heater, biasing factors were not identified; therefore,
no samples were taken.

For the Flare Stack Piping, biasing factors were not identified; therefore, no samples were taken.

Therefore, all of the sampling objectives specified in the DQOs were met for CAS 25-99-22,
Ancillary Facilities.

To satisfy the criteria that the analytical suite selected must be sufficient to identify any COCs present
in the samples at all CASs, the following criteria were met. The analytical methods were chosen
during the DQO process as the analyses required to detect any of the contaminants of potential
concern (COPCs) listed in the SAFER that were defined as the contaminants that could reasonably be
expected at the site that could contribute to a dose or risk exceeding FALs. The COPCs were
identified based on operational histories, waste inventories, release information, investigative
background, contaminant sources, release mechanisms, and migration pathways as presented in the
SAFER. This provides assurance that the analyses conducted for each sample are capable of
identifying any COPC present in the sample. All samples were analyzed using the analytical methods
listed in Section 3.1 of the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021).

Criterion 2 (Confidence in Detecting COCs Present in Samples)

Sample results were assessed against the acceptance criterion for the data quality indicator (DQI) of
sensitivity as defined in the Soils QAP (DOE/EMNV, 2017; Navarro, 2022). The sensitivity
acceptance criterion is that analytical detection limits will be less than the corresponding FAL
(DOE/EMNV, 2021). All of the chemical analyses met this criterion. For radionuclides, the criterion
is that all detection limits are less than their corresponding Occasional Use Area dose residual
radioactive material guidelines (RRMGs). All of the analytical result detection limits for every
radionuclide were less than their corresponding RRMGs. Therefore, the DQI for sensitivity has been
met for all contaminants, and no data were rejected due to sensitivity.

                                                                                         Page 39 of 48

Criterion 3 (Confidence that Dataset is of Sufficient Quality and Complete)

To satisfy the third criterion, the dataset was assessed against the acceptance criteria for the DQIs of
precision, accuracy, comparability, completeness, and representativeness, as defined in the Soils QAP
(DOE/EMNV, 2017; Navarro, 2022). The DQI acceptance criteria are presented in Table 6-1 of the
SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021). The individual DQI results are presented in the following subsections.

Precision
Precision was evaluated as described in Section 6.2.4 of the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) and
Section 4.2 of the Soils QAP (DOE/EMNV, 2017; Navarro, 2022). No constituents for any sample in
the CAU 572 dataset were qualified for precision. Therefore, the SAFER criterion for precision was
met for all contaminants. The potential for a false-negative DQO decision error is negligible, and the
results can be confidently used for decision making.

Accuracy
Accuracy was evaluated as described in Section 7.2.2 of the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) and
Section 4.2 of the Soils QAP (DOE/EMNV, 2017; Navarro, 2022). The sample results that were
qualified for accuracy are presented in Table 3-2. As stipulated in Section 4.3 of the Soils QAP, when
analyses of a particular contaminant do not meet the DQI criteria and the highest reported activity for
that contaminant exceeds one-half of its corresponding FAL, the data assessment must include
explanations or justifications for their use or rejection.

 Table 3-2
 Accuracy Measurements
 Number of Number of Percent
 Constituent Analyses Measurements Measurements within
 Qualified Performed Criteria

     Cadmium                                         9                   17                   53
 Metals
 Selenium 9 17 53

There were no analytical data qualified for accuracy that exceeded one-half the FAL. Of the sample
results qualified for cadmium, the highest reported concentration was 0.02 milligrams per kilogram
(mg/kg). The FAL for cadmium is 2,500 mg/kg. Of the sample results qualified for selenium, the
highest reported concentration was 5.1 mg/kg. The FAL for selenium is 146,000 mg/kg. Therefore,

                                                                                        Page 40 of 48

the SAFER criteria were met for contaminants. The potential for a false-negative DQO decision error
is negligible, and use of the results that were qualified for accuracy can be confidently used. The
dataset is therefore determined to be acceptable for the DQI of accuracy.

Representativeness
The DQO process as identified in Appendix B of the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) was used to
address sampling and analytical requirements for CAU 572. During this process, appropriate
locations were selected that enabled the samples collected to be representative of the population
parameters identified in the DQO (the most likely locations to contain contamination [judgmental
sampling]) (Section D.2.1). However, the sample results do not represent soil contamination levels, as
the samples were not collected from soil. They do represent contamination levels contained in
building materials or drain contents that have not yet been released to the soil. When these materials
contain contaminant concentrations that exceed a soil FAL, they are assumed to be PSM and require
corrective action.

Based on the methodical selection of sample locations, the analytical data acquired during the
CAU 572 CAI are considered to adequately represent contaminant concentrations of the sampled
population (building materials or drain contents).

Comparability
Field sampling, as described in the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021), was performed and documented in
accordance with approved procedures that are comparable to standard industry practices.
Approved analytical methods and procedures per DOE were used to analyze, report, and validate the
data. These are comparable to other methods used in industry and government practices, but most
importantly are comparable to other investigations conducted for the NNSS. Therefore, CAU 572
datasets are considered comparable to other datasets generated using these same standardized DOE
procedures, thereby meeting DQO requirements.

Also, standard approved field and analytical methods ensured that data were appropriate for
comparison to the investigation action levels specified in the SAFER.

                                                                                            Page 41 of 48

Completeness

The SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) defines acceptable criteria for completeness to be that the dataset is
sufficiently complete to be able to make the DQO decisions. This is initially evaluated as 80 percent
of release-specific analytes identified in the SAFER having valid results. Rejected data (either
qualified as rejected or data that failed the criterion of sensitivity) are not used in the resolution of
DQO decisions and are not counted toward meeting the completeness acceptance criterion.

As no data in the dataset for CAU 572 were rejected, sufficient information is available to make the
DQO decisions.

4.1.2.2 DQO Provisions To Limit False-Positive Decision Error

The false-positive decision error was controlled by assessing the potential for false-positive analytical
results. QA/quality control (QC) samples such as method blanks were used to determine whether a
false-positive analytical result may have occurred. This provision is evaluated during the data
validation process and appropriate qualifications are applied to the data when applicable. There were
no data qualifications that would indicate a potential false-positive analytical result.

Proper decontamination of sampling equipment also minimized the potential for cross-contamination
that could lead to a false-positive analytical result.

4.1.3 Decision II

Decision II as presented in the SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) is as follows: “Is sufficient information
available to evaluate potential CAAs?” Sufficient information is defined to include the following:

 • Identifying the volume of waste containing any COC or PSM.
 • The information needed to characterize waste for disposal.

A corrective action will be determined for any site containing a COC. The evaluation of the need for
corrective action will include the potential for wastes present at the site to cause the future
contamination of site environment media if the wastes were to be released.

                                                                                        Page 42 of 48

The information needed to predict potential remediation waste types and volumes and information
needed to evaluate the feasibility of remediation alternatives was provided by the analytical results
from soil samples and the identification of metallic lead.

4.1.4 Sampling Design

The SAFER (DOE/EMNV, 2021) stipulated that the following sampling process would
be implemented:

 • Judgmental sampling will be conducted at locations of potential contamination identified
 during the CAI.

 Result. Judgmental sampling was conducted as specified in the SAFER.

4.2 Conduct a Preliminary Data Review

A preliminary data review was conducted by reviewing QA reports and inspecting the data.
The contract analytical laboratories generate a QA nonconformance report when data quality
does not meet contractual requirements. All data received from the analytical laboratories met
contractual requirements, and a QA nonconformance report was not generated. Data were validated
and verified to ensure that the measurement systems performed in accordance with the criteria
specified in the Soils QAP (DOE/EMNV, 2017; Navarro, 2022). The validated dataset quality was
found to be satisfactory.

4.3 Select the Test and Identify Key Assumptions

The test for making DQO decisions for radiological contamination was the comparison of the TED to
the FAL of 25 mrem/OU-yr. For other types of contamination, the test for making DQO decisions was
the comparison of the maximum analyte result from each release to the corresponding FAL.
All radiological FALs were based on an exposure duration to a site worker using the Occasional Use
Area exposure scenario. All chemical FALs, except for lead, were based on an exposure duration to a
site worker using the Industrial Area exposure scenario. The FAL for lead was based on an exposure
duration to a site worker using the Remote Work Area exposure scenario.

                                                                                      Page 43 of 48

4.3.1   CAS 25-33-01, Building 3220, Equipment Building

Based on the results of samples collected at CAS 25-33-01 listed in Table H.1-3, no analytical result
exceeded a FAL for radiological or chemical contamination.

4.3.2 CAS 25-33-02, Building 3230, Motor Drive Building

Based on the results of samples collected at CAS 25-33-02 listed in Table H.1-3, lead contamination
is present in the drains at levels that exceed the FAL.

4.3.3   CAS 25-33-03, Building 3231, Pump House

No biasing factors were discovered during the CAI at this CAS and no samples were needed.
Therefore, it was determined that no COCs are present at this CAS.

4.3.4 CAS 25-33-04, Building 3232, Cryogenic Evaluation Lab

Based on the results of samples collected at CAS 25-33-04 listed in Table H.1-3, arsenic, chromium,
and lead contamination is present in paint chip samples at levels that exceed the FALs.

4.3.5 CAS 25-99-22, Ancillary Facilities

Based on the results of samples collected at CAS 25-99-22 listed in Table H.1-3, three samples
contained contamination that exceeded a FAL. A sediment sample from cooling tower exceeded the
FAL for arsenic. A paint chip sample from Dewar 1 exceeded the FALs for chromium and PCBs. A
paint chip sample from the borated water storage tank exceeded the FALs for PCBs and semivolatile
organic compounds (SVOCs).

4.4 Verify the Assumptions

The results of the investigation support the key assumptions identified in the CAU 572 DQOs.
All data collected during the CAI supported the CSM, and no revisions to the CSM were necessary.

4.5 Draw Conclusions from the Data

The following subsections resolve the two DQO decisions for each of the CAU 572 CASs.

                                                                                      Page 44 of 48

4.5.1 Resolution of DQO Decisions

Decision I

The DQO decision on the presence of COCs was resolved based on the presence of PSM.
PSM was found in the paint and drains at some of the CAU 572 CASs. Staining was visually
observed in some of the below-ground vaults and was assumed to be hydrocarbon. Also, metallic lead
was found during the CAI in cracks between concrete pads near Dewars 1 and 2. Therefore, it was
determined that corrective actions are required for PSM at some of the CAU 572 CASs.

Decision II

Decision II for the paint at CAU 572 was resolved by determining the presence of PSM utilizing
visual observation and analytical data results. Paints at three of the five CASs include PSM and when
released could exceed FAL. The boundary of the area that requires corrective action was defined as
the line encompassing the physical extent of CAU 572 associated with each of these CASs.

Appendix G. The UR signs for CAU 572 will be placed on the TCC fence.

 Appendix A

 DQOs as Developed in the SAFER
Note: This appendix contains the DQOs presented in SAFER and consists of
 Appendix B of the SAFER. Therefore, cross-references, page numbers, and
 header information in this appendix refer to the original document.

 Page B-1 of B-32

B.1.0 Introduction

The DQO process described in this appendix is a seven-step strategic systematic planning method
used to plan data collection activities and define performance criteria for the CAU 572, Test Cell C
Ancillary Building and Structures, field investigation. The DQOs are designed to ensure that the data
collected will provide sufficient and reliable information to determine the appropriate corrective
actions, to verify the adequacy of existing information, to provide sufficient data to implement the
corrective actions, and to verify that closure was achieved.

The CAU 572 CAI will be based on the DQOs presented in this appendix as developed by
representatives of NDEP and EM Nevada Program. The seven steps of the DQO process presented in
Sections B.2.0 through B.8.0 were developed in accordance with the Guidance on Systematic
Planning Using the Data Quality Objectives Process (EPA, 2006) and the CAS-specific information
presented in Section B.2.0.

The DQO process presents a judgmental sampling approach. In general, the procedures used in the
DQO process provide the following:

 • A method to establish performance or acceptance criteria, which serve as the basis for
 designing a plan for collecting data of sufficient quality and quantity to support the goals of
       a study (EPA, 2002).

 • Criteria that will be used to establish the final data collection design such as:

 - The nature of the problem that has initiated the study and a conceptual model of the
 environmental hazard to be investigated.

 - The decisions or estimates that need to be made and the order of priority for
 resolving them.

 - The type of data needed.

 - An analytic approach or decision rule that defines the logic for how the data will be used to
 draw conclusions from the study findings.

 • Acceptable quantitative criteria on the quality and quantity of the data to be collected, relative
 to the ultimate use of the data.

 Page B-2 of B-32

• A data collection design that will generate data meeting the quantitative and qualitative
 criteria specified. A data collection design specifies the type, number, location, and physical
 quantity of samples and data, as well as the QA and QC activities that will ensure that
 sampling design and measurement errors are managed sufficiently to meet the performance or
 acceptance criteria specified in the DQOs (see Section B.8.1.2).

 Page B-3 of B-32

B.2.0 Step 1 - State the Problem

Step 1 of the DQO process defines the problem that requires study, identifies the planning team, and
develops a conceptual model of the environmental hazard to be investigated.

The problem statement for CAU 572 is: “Existing information on the nature and extent of potential
contamination is insufficient to evaluate and confirm closure of the CASs in CAU 572.” CAU 572
comprises CAS 25-33-01, Building 3220, Equipment Building; CAS 25-33-02, Building 3230,
Motor Drive Building; CAS 25-33-03, Building 3231, Pump House; CAS 25-33-04, Building 3232,
Cryogenic Evaluation Lab; and CAS 25-99-22, Ancillary Facilities. These CASs potentially contain
COCs in environmental media or PSM that could cause the release of a COC to environmental media.

B.2.1 Planning Team Members

The DQO planning team consists of representatives from NDEP and EM Nevada Program.
The DQO meeting was held on July 14, 2020. The primary decision makers are the NDEP and
EM Nevada Program.

B.2.2 Conceptual Site Model

The CSM is used to organize and communicate information about site characteristics. It reflects the
best interpretation of available information at any point in time. The CSM is a primary vehicle for
communicating assumptions about release mechanisms, potential migration pathways, or specific
constraints. It provides a summary of how and where contaminants are expected to move and what
impacts such movement may have. It is the basis for assessing how contaminants could reach
receptors both in the present and future. The CSM describes the most probable scenario for current
conditions at each site and define the assumptions that are the basis for identifying appropriate
sampling strategy and data collection methods. Accurate CSMs are important as they serve as the
basis for all subsequent inputs and decisions throughout the DQO process.

The CSM was developed for CAU 572 using information from the physical setting, potential
contaminant sources, release information, historical background information, knowledge from similar
sites, and physical and chemical properties of the potentially affected media and COPCs.

 Page B-4 of B-32

The CSM consists of the following:

 • Potential contaminant releases associated with the CASs in CAU 572.

 • Release mechanisms (the conditions associated with the release).

 • Potential contaminant source characteristics, including contaminants suspected to be present
 and contaminant-specific properties.

 • Site characteristics including physical, topographical, and meteorological information.

 • Migration pathways and transport mechanisms that describe the potential for migration and
 where the contamination may be transported.

 • The locations of points of exposure where individuals or populations may come in contact
 with a COC associated with a CAS.

 • Routes of exposure where contaminants may enter the receptor.

If additional elements are identified during the CAI that are outside the scope of the CSM, the
situation will be reviewed, and a recommendation will be made as to how to proceed. In such
cases, NDEP will be notified and given the opportunity to comment on, and concur with,
the recommendation.

The applicability of the CSM is summarized in Table B.2-1 and discussed below. Table B.2-1
provides information on CSM elements that will be used throughout the remaining steps of the
DQO process.

B.2.2.1 Contaminant Release

Based on process knowledge and previous surveys and inspections, the potential for contamination to
be present that would exceed COC or PSM criteria is very low. These buildings were designed for
industrial type work activities in support of the radiological experiments and tests that were
conducted at other locations at Test Cell C. The primary design of the investigation will be to use
visual and radiological surveys to identify biasing factors that indicate the potential presence of
contamination. Samples will be collected at locations determined by the surveys to have the
maximum contamination. In the absence of any biasing factors, samples will not be collected.

B.2.2.3 Contaminant Characteristics

Contaminant characteristics include, but are not limited to, solubility, density, and adsorption
potential. In general, contaminants with large particle size, low solubility, high affinity for media,
and/or high density can be expected to be found relatively close to release points. Contaminants with
small particle size, high solubility, low affinity for media, and/or low density are found farther from
release points or in low areas where evaporation of ponding will concentrate dissolved constituents.

Based on process knowledge, the radiological contaminants are expected to be those associated with
nuclear fission and activation. Those radionuclides generally have a strong gamma signature that can
be readily detected using survey instruments.

B.2.2.4 Site Characteristics

Site characteristics are defined by the interaction of physical, topographical, and meteorological
attributes and properties. Physical properties include permeability, porosity, hydraulic conductivity,
degree of saturation, sorting, chemical composition, and organic content. Topographical and
meteorological properties and attributes include slope stability, precipitation frequency and

 Page B-7 of B-32

amounts, precipitation runoff pathways, drainage channels and ephemeral streams, and
evapotranspiration potential.

The CAU 572 sites are located in Jackass Flats in Area 25 of the NNSS. Jackass Flats is between
Yucca Mountain on the west and southwest and Little Skull Mountain to the south. The Calico Hills
are directly north, Mid Valley and Lookout Peak are to the northeast, and Skull Mountain is to the
southeast. Jackass Flats is a broad alluvial valley with alluvium and colluvium accumulations up to
1,205 ft (USGS, 1964; DOE, 1988). The alluvium in Jackass Flats is underlain by welded and
semi-welded ash-flow and ash-fall tuffs of Tertiary age. Beneath the tuff layers lie Paleozoic
carbonate and clastic sediments with a depth of up to 22,000 ft in some areas. The Paleozoic rocks are
made up of shales, quartzites, and carbonates of lower to middle Cambrian age; carbonate and thin
shale layers of middle Cambrian to Devonian age; and argillites, cherty limestones, and
conglomerates of Devonian to Permian age (SNPO, 1970).

Elevation of the flats ranges from 3,600 ft in the north to 3,200 ft in the south, with the Test Cell C
Facility at 3,520 ft. Surface water flow at the north end of the Test Cell C Facility drains to the
southwest; at the south end of the facility, surface water drains to the south. The nearest natural water
source is Topopah Springs at the head of Topopah Wash 8.7 miles to the north. The closest well to the
site is J-11 Water Well, which is located approximately 9,500 ft southeast of the Test Cell C Facility.
The depth to groundwater as measured from this well is approximately 1,040 ft below ground surface
(bgs) (DRI, 1996; USGS and DOE, 2020).

B.2.2.5 Migration Pathways and Transport Mechanisms

Migration pathways include the lateral migration of potential contaminants across surface
soils/sediments and vertical migration of potential contaminants through subsurface soils.

The Test Cell C Facility is toward the middle of Jackass Flats, about 500 ft west of Topopah Wash.
Fortymile Wash, the major drainage in the area, meanders along the east base of Yucca Mountain and
the west side of Jackass Flats, and eventually joins with the Amargosa River to the south. Topopah
Wash, originating in the Calico Hills, bisects Jackass Flats and also joins with the Amargosa River,
farther to the east (DRI, 1996). Contaminants released into the Topopah Wash are subject to much
higher transport mechanisms than contaminants released to other surface areas. Topopah Wash is

 Page B-8 of B-32

generally dry but is subject to infrequent, potentially intense, stormwater flows. These stormwater
flow events provide an intermittent mechanism for both vertical and horizontal transport of
contaminants. Contaminated sediments entrained by these stormwater events would be carried by the
streamflow to locations where the flowing water loses energy and the sediments drop out. These
locations are readily identifiable by hydrologists as sedimentation areas.

Infiltration and percolation of precipitation serves as a driving force for downward migration of
contaminants. However, due to the low permeability of the alluvium throughout the area, high
potential evapotranspiration rates, and low precipitation rates (approximately 5.72 inches per year as
measured from station 4JA [ARL/SORD, 2020]), percolation of infiltrated precipitation at the NNSS
does not provide a significant mechanism for vertical migration of contaminants to groundwater
(DOE/NV, 1992). Environmental contamination is, therefore, expected to be limited to the area near
release points.

B.2.2.6 Land-Use and Exposure Scenarios

Human receptors may be exposed to COPCs through oral ingestion, inhalation, or dermal contact
(absorption) of soil or debris due to inadvertent disturbance of these materials, or irradiation by
radioactive materials. The land-use and exposure scenarios for CAU 572 are listed in Table B.2-3.
These are based on NNSS current and future land use (DOE/NV, 1998). Although Test Cell C is
located in an area where structures from past activities exist, no facilities are present that would allow
these to be used as an assigned work station for NNSS site personnel; therefore, Test Cell C is
considered an occasional use area.

                                            Table B.2-3

B.3.0 Step 2 - Identify the Goal of the Study

Step 2 of the DQO process states how environmental data will be used in meeting objectives and
solving the problem, identifies study questions or decision statement(s), and considers alternative
outcomes or actions that can occur upon answering the question(s). Figure B.3-1 depicts the
sequential flow of questions, answers, and action alternatives required to fulfill the objectives of the
SAFER process.

B.3.1 Decision Statements

The Decision I statement is: “Are any COCs present or is any waste present at the site likely to result
in the introduction of COCs into site environmental media?” If a COC is detected, then Decision II
must be resolved.

The Decision II statement is: “Is sufficient information available to meet the closure objectives?”
Sufficient information to meet these closure objectives is defined to include:

 • Identifying the volume of waste containing any COC or PSM.
 • The information needed to characterize waste for disposal.

As presented in the CAU 572 DQOs and as described in Section B.8.0, all of the sampling is
judgmental based on defined populations of PSM. Therefore, the extent of contamination is defined
as the entire PSM, and no extent sampling is required unless soil contamination is discovered. A
corrective action will be necessary if COCs are present or there is a potential for wastes that are
present at a site to result in the introduction of COCs into site environmental media. These wastes
would be considered PSM, which is defined as waste (solid or liquid) containing contaminants that, if
released to soil, would result in soil contamination exceeding a FAL. To determine whether wastes
that are present at CAU 572 meet the criteria for PSM, the following conservative assumptions
were made:

 • Any containment of waste (e.g., fuel/oil reservoirs, pipe, concrete vaults and walls, drums)
 would fail at some point, and the waste would be released to the surrounding soil.

 • A waste, regardless of concentration or configuration, may be assumed to be PSM and
 handled under a corrective action.

                                                                                          Page B-10 of B-32

 • Based on process knowledge and/or professional judgment, some waste may be assumed not
 PSM if it is clear that it could not result in soil contamination exceeding a FAL.

 • If assumptions about the waste cannot be made, then the waste material will be sampled, and
 the results will be compared to FALs based on the following criteria:

 - For non-liquid wastes, the concentration of any chemical contaminant in soil
 (following degradation of the waste and release of contaminants into soil) would be equal
 to the concentration of the chemical contaminant in the waste (no consideration will be
 given to dilution into the mass of soil).

 - For non-liquid wastes, the dose resulting from radioactive contaminants in soil
 (following degradation of the waste and release of contaminants into soil) would be
 calculated using the activity concentration of the contaminant (for each radioactive
 contaminant) and calculating the combined resulting dose using the RESRAD code
 (Murphy, 2004) (no consideration will be given to dilution into the mass of soil).
 Note: As an initial screening tool, if building materials are primarily externally
 contaminated and do not present a dose exceeding the FAL to a nearby worker in its current
 configuration, it will not be considered to meet PSM criteria.

 - For liquid wastes, the resulting concentration of contaminants in the surrounding soil
 would be calculated based on the concentration of contaminants in the wastes and the
 liquid holding capacity of the soil.

For example, sludge containing a contaminant exceeding an equivalent FAL concentration would be
considered to be PSM and would require a corrective action. Light ballasts with capacitors are
assumed to contain PCBs based on process knowledge. These ballasts/capacitors would be assumed
to be PSM without sampling and would require a corrective action.

If sufficient information is not available to meet the closure objectives, then site conditions will be
re-evaluated, and additional samples will be collected (as long as the scope of the CAI is not exceeded
and any CSM assumption has not been shown to be incorrect).
